So when you look at anything within your program, really, if you aren't achieving reps close to failure, sets close to failure and a good amount of work. Siding with a little bit less is a smart move because you can always add. More volume, but taking away, you might by doing so much lead to your quality being sacrificed, whether that be your form or how hard you're pushing and your form and how hard you're pushing is so fundamentally the quality of stimulus that you're trying to provide to give your muscles in impetus to grow. So it's so important that you hold on to that with that fundamental principle being quality first, always, then you can always add on top of that. And also knowing that it's diminishing returns past meeting that threshold, right? You kind of meet the growth threshold and then it's like, hey, you add more, but you don't just get exponentially more or even linearly more, you get less and less. So that investment might not even be worth it. But if you don't have the quality there first, you can add all you want, but you may not even grow if you don't meet the threshold for taking a muscle close to failure. So then what is just something inherently you can use within your own physiology to know if you've trained hard enough is do I feel like I could handle more of my feeling quite beat up? Like by the end of that workout where I've trained that muscle group, does it feel pretty tired and fatigued? And I feel like I've like given it a good go. The following day, am I feeling like I trained that muscle? It might be a little bit of nagging soreness. It might just feel like it's a bit tired or fatigued. So I feel like I've given it a good shot. And then am I in a round that 10 sets, like kind of lower threshold? It could be for some lower body muscles, like many of you might know your hamstrings just can't tolerate as much volume. It's very common for people that might be closer to six. So I think as long as you're kind of know you're in around that ballpark, you know, you're kind of meeting these minimum thresholds. And then over time, am I progressing? And if you are progressing, that's the ultimate arbitrary. You growing really in the kind of hypertrophy rep range that is like not your one rep max. But am I adding reps or load to this kind of five to 30 repetition spectrum? If you're able to do that, you know, you're giving your body sufficient stresses to cause it to respond, adapt. And the reason you can lift more is because you've grown muscle tissue to be able to do that. For someone more advanced, it's going to take much more time versus someone more novice. I don't want to get too much into the weeds with this. I already felt like I did a little bit there with some of that discussion. I just wanted that to be like real world, practical. What matters for building muscle when you're going to the gym and you're training where it be like a chest session, a pull session, legs, full body. You need to take the muscles close to failure. So you need to pick exercises that allow you to do that. And you need to make sure you get into repetitions that really challenge you. So you're trying to do a forceful concentric, but it slows down involuntary. You may even take it to the point where you're like, Hey, I couldn't do anymore. You literally hit a concentric repetition and you couldn't do it. That could happen to you need to meet that threshold. You need to train sufficiently hard. And then you need to make sure that you're doing enough. So you need to feel challenged by the end of that workout. That muscle feels like it had a good go and that you have some potential buyer feedback that you've done sufficient work following days afterwards. And then you basically want to train it again once it's feeling fresh and ready to go again. It doesn't have to feel 100% but feel good enough. And so long you're in that ballpark, then you know, Hey, I'm meeting these thresholds for growth. If I want to lean into a bit more from feeling like, Hey, I feel pretty be up. I could handle a bit more, maybe put a bit more in there, but quality first always. So thresholds training hard enough with enough and then quality has to always be there. And if you keep meeting these by default, you're progressively overloading because it won't be hard if you don't increase the load at some point or the repetitions at some point, because it will start moving out of this threshold because your body will adapt. And I think that's really where I want this video to end. I don't again, I want these videos to be kind of a bit shorter, bit snappier, give you the fundamental knowledge that you need to take home to feel like confident in what you're doing. Because I know for so many years I was like, am I doing the right thing? And I think sometimes I missed the forest for the trees.
